Stop teaching leadership. Start letting people practise it

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Managers raised on interactive games are still trained through slides and scripted workshops. Immersive simulations let them practise conflict, judgement and difficult conversations before real teams pay the price.

Organizations are preparing a generation raised on digital technology and interactive games to lead in a world of complexity. Yet many still train managers through presentations and scripted workshops. This is a costly mistake. If we want leaders who can navigate conflict, uncertainty, and human behavior, we must stop telling them how to lead.

We must give them opportunities to practise it.

I have spent decades exploring how emotional intelligence works in real situations. I believe leadership development is failing to keep pace with the people it needs to develop.

Emerging managers are accustomed to exploring possibilities and testing decisions. They are used to learning from failure and adapting when circumstances change. Yet traditional training too often delivers predetermined answers. It measures success by attendance or course completion. Real workplaces demand something far more challenging.

This is not about adding points, badges, or competitions to existing courses. Gamification adds game-like rewards to an activity. Immersive learning goes further. It uses interactive scenarios, role-play, meaningful objectives, and decisions with consequences to develop practical skills.

A corporate target becomes a quest. A key performance indicator becomes a challenge. It requires people to allocate resources, collaborate, assess incomplete information, and change direction when their first attempt fails.

Consider a character whose special power is generating fire. Used carefully, it can solve a problem. Used recklessly, it destroys everything around it, including the people the character needs. Leadership strengths work the same way.

Confidence becomes arrogance when unchecked. Analytical thinking becomes paralysis when taken too far. Empathy becomes avoidance when it prevents difficult conversations. Immersive scenarios allow people to discover these consequences before their behavior damages a real team.

The need is becoming more urgent as AI transforms work. Harvard Business Impact’s 2026 Global Leadership Study found that 53% of respondents expected leaders to make greater use of AI in strategic decision-making this year. As these tools become more influential, leaders must become better at exercising judgment, understanding people, and taking responsibility for decisions.

AI can analyze data and recommend actions. It cannot replace the human responsibility to understand how those actions affect others.

This approach has an academic foundation. A 2026 study published in the International Journal of Serious Games examined a live role-play simulation designed for school leaders. Feedback from 72 principals and vice principals highlighted opportunities to explore evidence-informed decisions through complex scenarios, feedback, and reflection. The findings support taking immersive learning seriously as a tool for leadership development.

Researchers studying role-playing also use the term “bleed”. It describes how emotions, attitudes, and aspects of identity can spill over between a character and the person playing it. A 2024 paper published in the International Journal of Role-Playing offers a useful perspective on workplace learning. It explored how these experiences can influence a player’s sense of self.

Practicing assertiveness in a simulated conversation, for example, may help someone feel more prepared to speak up in a difficult meeting. Whether that confidence carries into the workplace depends on the individual and the learning environment. It also depends on opportunities to apply and reflect on the experience.

Organizations that ignore this opportunity risk producing managers who can recite leadership principles but struggle when relationships become difficult. As AI takes on more routine work, these workplaces could become increasingly automated, impersonal, and disconnected from the people they serve.

Organizations that embrace immersive development can create opportunities for teams to practise adaptability, collaboration, and creative problem-solving. Teams can also learn to value different personalities and cultural perspectives.

I am in my late 60s. I believe experienced leaders have a responsibility to listen to younger managers rather than dismiss how they learn. If you lead an organization, stop treating immersive development as an optional experiment. Replace one conventional training session with a realistic simulation.

Give participants conflicting priorities, limited information, and a difficult conversation to navigate. Let them try, fail, reflect, and try again. Then assess whether their behavior changes at work.

We do not need to abandon established leadership principles. We need to stop teaching them in ways that leave people unprepared to use them. The future is human. As AI advances, our success will depend on empathy, emotional awareness, sound judgment, and meaningful interaction.

The organizations that thrive will be those that teach people not simply to perform like machines, but to lead like humans.

Dr. Robin Hills is a specialist in emotional intelligence and the director of Ei4Change, an emotional intelligence learning and development (show all) Dr. Robin Hills is a specialist in emotional intelligence and the director of Ei4Change, an emotional intelligence learning and development organization based in Greater Manchester, United Kingdom. His work draws on decades of applied business experience and research-informed approaches to psychology, neuroscience, and positive psychology. He focuses on helping individuals and organizations translate emotional intelligence into practical skills for leadership, communication, decision-making, resilience, and collaboration in a changing world for professionals and teams.

The chips are down: Qualcomm falls on revenue miss, weak guidance; Arm and GlobalFoundries do little better

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Chipmakers Qualcomm Inc., Arm Holdings Ltd. and GlobalFoundries Ltd. struggled to move the needle today after reporting financial results that clearly left investors wanting more.

After an impressive earnings beat from Advanced Micro Devices Inc. just 24 hours earlier, investors may have had heightened expectations. But if they did, on a day when the market rocketed higher on the U.S. election results, they were clearly let down by the shaky performance of Qualcomm, which beat expectations on earnings but missed on revenue and offered weak guidance. The result was predictable, with its stock down more than 6% after-hours.

Both Arm and GlobalFoundries did better, but it wasn’t enough to satisfy investors who are becoming increasingly concerned about the sustainability of the artificial intelligence industry that has driven significant growth for all three chipmakers over the last couple of years.

The biggest disappointment was Qualcomm, which reported fourth-quarter earnings before certain costs such as stock compensation of $2.17 per share on revenue of $10.44 billion, up just 8% from a year earlier. The results were mixed, with Wall Street looking for earnings of $2.16 per share on sales of $10.48 billion. For the current quarter, Qualcomm said it’s looking for earnings of around $2.25 per share at the midpoint of its guidance range on sales of $10.48 billion, falling short of the Street’s target of $2.27 per share in earnings and $10.48 billion in revenue.

Qualcomm’s troubles stem from the fact that it has less exposure to the AI industry’s rampant growth than many other chipmakers. The bulk of its sales comes from system-on-chips and modems for smartphones. But the market is currently reeling due to the rising cost of components such as memory, which have forced handset makers to bump up their prices, reducing demand. Qualcomm has tried to weather the storm by raising the prices of its own chips recently, but its smartphone chip sales fell 26% in the quarter to just $5.14 billion, well behind the Street’s target of $5.21 billion.

Under Chief Executive Cristiano Amon (pictured), Qualcomm has tried to pivot and grow its position in alternative chip markets, particularly the AI data center industry. Revenue from that segment rose to $650 million during the quarter, but it’s still dwarfed by the smartphone business.

Nonetheless, Amon insisted that the company will grow its data center business to more than $5 billion in revenue by the end of fiscal 2027. He said the company recently struck a deal with an unnamed global hyperscaler for optical connectivity chips. “We don’t have a demand issue; it’s a supply issue,” he told analysts on a conference call. “If I had more supply, I would probably be doing better.”

Qualcomm’s automotive business was a bright spot, with sales there rising 89% to $1.99 billion in the quarter, above the Street’s consensus estimate of $1.66 billion. Internet of Things revenue came to $1.88 billion, up 4% from a year ago and just ahead of the Street’s estimate.

Finally, there’s Qualcomm’s QTL business, which generates revenue from licensing intellectual property for modems, 5G connectivity and other technologies. That segment added $1.3 billion in revenue, down 7%, despite signing renewed licensing agreements with Apple Inc., Transsion Inc. and Huawei Technologies Co. Ltd.

Chip design company Arm delivered much more palatable results, but it was somewhat cautious with its guidance for the current quarter, and its stock was down 2% in late trading today. The company reported adjusted earnings of 52 cents per share, just beating the Street’s target of 48 cents, while revenue rose 27% from a year earlier, to $1.44 billion, ahead of the $1.38 billion analyst estimate.

Unlike Qualcomm, Arm doesn’t manufacture complete chips for its customers, but instead designs and licenses processor blueprints for customers such as Apple Inc., Samsung Electronics Co. Ltd. and Qualcomm to build their own custom chips. Where Arm is more similar to Qualcomm is in its strategy, where it too is also pivoting to focus on data center chip designs, trying to tap into the demand for AI compute. Arm said its data center business was the main growth driver this year, with royalties there doubling year-over-year.

However, Arm’s guidance did little to excite investors. The company said it’s looking for earnings of 56 cents per share, plus or minus four cents, compared with Wall Street’s forecast of 55 cents per share. For revenue, it’s targeting $1.54 billion, plus or minus $50 million, which is just ahead of the Street’s $1.52 billion target.

GlobalFoundries did a little better than its peers today, posting results and guidance for the current that squeaked past expectations. However, its stock remained flat after-hours.

The company reported third-quarter adjusted earnings of 53 cents per share on sales of $1.9 billion, up 12% from a year earlier, with growth driven by strong demand from AI and data center customers, it said. The results were better than expected, with analysts targeting earnings of 51 cents on revenue of $1.89 billion.

GlobalFoundries’ also differs from Qualcomm, in that it’s a pure-play contract manufacturer that builds the processors designed by semiconductors firms, rather than designing chips itself or selling them under its own brand. Qualcomm is actually one of its biggest customers, alongside Broadcom Inc., NXP Semiconductors N.V. and Infineon Technologies Inc. As such, its fortunes are very much aligned with those of the chip designers it caters too.

For the current quarter, GlobalFoundries said it’s looking for earnings of 59 cents per share, plus or minus five cents, on revenue of about $2 billion. Wall Street is looking for earnings of 57 cents per share and has the same revenue target.

Amazon, Microsoft and other tech giants close higher after Democrats’ midterm sweep

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Shares of Amazon.com Inc., Microsoft Corp., Alphabet Inc. and Meta Platforms Inc. closed higher today following Democrats’ resounding midterm victory.

Amazon jumped 8.3% at one point and ended the trading session 6.1% higher than Tuesday. Microsoft gained nearly 6.7% while Alphabet and Google rose by about 3.1% and 1.4%, respectively. Multiple industry analysts tied the stock gains to the companies’ artificial intelligence buildout.

Democrats won both chambers of Congress on a campaign that emphasized data center restrictions. Furthermore, voters approved 40 ballot measures focused on blocking or limiting AI infrastructure projects. Bloomberg cited Robert Pavlik, a senior portfolio manager at Dakota Wealth Management, as saying that tech giants’ shares jumped because investors believe a data center slowdown could improve their finances.

Amazon, Meta and Google have become cash-flow-negative in recent months because of their steep infrastructure investments. Last Wednesday, the search giant reported a negative cash flow that exceeded the consensus estimate by about $6.4 billion. Google’s stock price plummeted 10% in after-hours trading even though its cloud revenue more than doubled.

Needham & Co., B. ‌Riley ⁠Wealth and prominent tech industry analyst Ben Thompson highlighted another possible contributor to the stock rally. New restrictions on AI projects would make computing infrastructure more scarce, which would boost the value of existing data centers. That would benefit the tech giants that logged share price gains today.

Thompson noted that neoclouds, or emerging AI-focused cloud providers, may also benefit in certain respects. Developments for which they have already secured regulatory permission could become more valuable. At the same time, they may find it more difficult to finance new sites.

That dynamic weighed on chip stocks today. Nvidia Corp. and Intel Corp. both shed more than 3% of their value, while Broadcom Inc. declined 2%. SK hynix Inc., the world’s largest supplier of HBM memory for graphics cards, dropped by more than 5%. One notable exception was Advanced Micro Devices Inc., which ended the trading day about 1% higher.

Thompson noted that the midterms could also affect the AI industry in other ways. He pointed out that a stricter regulatory environment may give space-based data centers a boost. Anthropic PBC and OpenAI Group PBC, in turn, could see their hardware costs decline if new restrictions are placed on frontier model training. Such a development may ease their path to profitability.

Last month, Meta announced plans to sell up to $95 billion in shares to finance new data centers. On Monday, Oracle Corp. filed to issue $20 billion worth of bonds and shares in connection with its own AI buildout. It’s currently unclear how the midterm results will affect those initiatives and other large-scale data center projects currently underway.

French Union Accuses Microsoft of Deadlocking Marvel’s Blade

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Michael Manning (He/Him) has been enamored with video games since he first played the Sonic Classic Collection as a child. With decades of experience in gaming and a full tour as a Gaming Journalist, Michael is ready to step up to the plate. From AAA to the smallest passion projects, his motto is that every piece of ar

Many studios have been in rough patches recently, either due to internal struggles amid the modern challenges of development or due to layoffs or closures mandated by larger owners. It's easy to write off every issue in the modern industry as someone else’s fault, but that’s not realistic; every part of the industry is struggling, inside and out.

The upcoming God of War spin-off has been delayed to May 20th, dodging the busy February release window.

Arkane Lyon is one studio that's been in hot water for a long while, struggling to find a buyer before Microsoft closes its doors. Following hopeful news as more buyers step up, a French union with ties to Arkane staff has accused Microsoft of attempting to sabotage the development of the studio’s current project, Blade, by barring hires for over a year.

The development of Marvel’s Blade has been absolutely hectic, between internal shifts in the game’s vision and an early announcement that put heat on Arkane far before they were ready. Now, STJV, the French union representing certain members of Arkane Lyon’s staff, has made a major accusation, claiming the game is coming along fine, despite Microsoft’s interference.

According to STJV in a direct quotation, “Today, as work on Blade is going fine, and the level of quality of the game to be expected is on [par] with what Arkane has accustomed its players, workers have to face the harsh reality: our studio might get closed because, and only because, Microsoft decided to, even if that means sabotaging the game it funded.”

This all still has yet to be confirmed, as no direct response from Microsoft has been released, despite the accusations levied by STJV. Alongside the biting remarks, the union has dropped a series of demands, including a say in who buys Arkane, and a larger payout for the staff if no buyer steps forward.

It's easy to write off every issue in the modern industry as someone else’s fault, but that’s not realistic, every part of the industry is struggling, inside and out.

Despite all of this swinging out, Arkane is still eager to release Blade, staying optimistic that the title will release in the following years if Arkane is allowed to keep their doors open. STJV keeps the argument practical, framing the studio’s progress as a financial incentive for both parties. “Even this closure itself is done against any financial logic, as closing Arkane today would probably be more expensive than finishing the game,” says STJV.

Arkane’s aim to finish the game despite their wild situation is comforting to see, and gives me real hope at the quality of their Blade title, as long as it manages to release. The whispers of big studios like Ubisoft’s interest make it clear more than just Arkane want to see the title released, and as the months continue to tick by with the company still open, those whispers just get louder.

Arkane is a studio that would feel like a serious loss for the larger gaming industry if they closed their doors, even after stumbles like Redfall. Dropping into the mainstream scene with hits like Dishonored and Prey, Arkane Lyon is infamous for worlds that feel so lived-in, even after devastation has raked across them.

They truly do feel like the perfect team to work on Blade, as their mastery of immersive sims and wild powers leaves massive room for the exact type of action Blade is perfect for. It really does feel like every production surrounding Blade is hexed, if the canceled films and Arkane’s own pains are anything to look at.

I’m very eager to hear a response from Microsoft, as STJV has levied some major accusations that could really disrupt how third parties and internal studios interact with them. At present, we only have STJV’s word to go off of, so take these accusations with a grain of salt until more comes out.

The Elder Scrolls 6 has finally had its title officially confirmed, weeks after it was not so subtly leaked to the wider public.

Di Canio: "Vi dico con chi ce l'aveva Politano! Ora facciano come l'Inter che è sana e cioè..."

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Di Canio: "Vi dico con chi ce l'aveva Politano! Ora facciano come l'Inter che è sana e cioè..."

"Mi piacerebbe ogni tanto venisse anche qualche altro compagno di squadra a dare qualche spiegazione, non sempre noi italiani. Ci metto la faccia come abbiamo sempre fatto". Queste le pesanti parole di Matteo Politano a Sky Sport dopo la debacle del Napoli sul campo del Porto.

Negli studi dell'emittente, Paolo Di Canio, ex calciatore, ha commentato così le parole dell'esterno: "Abbiamo vissuto tutti momenti di scazzo dopo una partita, ma non vai a puntare direttamente gli stranieri: non credo possa parlare di Hojlund, abbiamo tutti la percezione dell'impegno, non puoi imputargli nulla.

Quello che sta creando un po' di cose è un po' De Bruyne, che si crede superiore al contesto dove si trova perché se sta fuori è lesa maestà, dopo ci parli e lo rimetti in carreggiata. Arriva Politano quasi non pensando alla sconfitta ad indicare questa cosa. I giocatori devono trovare una soluzione come fa l'Inter, che è sana come società: quando non va qualcosa, si trovano perché hanno gli anticorpi, si guardano in faccia e ricominciano a correre nella stessa direzione".

World of Warcraft: Forever, Warhammer Survivors, and a batch of fresh updates.

I can't ignore the elephant in the room: Grand Theft Auto 6 is out this month, but we still don't know when we'll see Rockstar's return trip to Vice City launch on PC. When we asked in a poll whether y'all planned to wait for the PC release, play it on console at launch, or double dip and do both, a whopping 71% said you would wait. Modding is half the fun for me, so I get it. I'm with you.

But even PC releases are dodging the big GTA 6-shaped gap in November, so what new games are even out there for you to play? Well, World of Warcraft: Forever kicks off the beginning of the month if you need a new MMO to get sucked into. Then Warhammer Survivors is just a few days later, and there's a whole slew of updates to pick through. It may be a light month, but it's not empty.

I'm eyeing a few November releases myself, though I wouldn't mind taking the time to catch up on the 2026 games I missed. As for recommendations, I'll let my colleagues take it from here.

Battle.net | November 4 World of Warcraft is 22 years old, which is bananas, and somehow I've gone all that time without touching it once. I've reported on it extensively (although not so much these days), but actually played it? Nope. With World of Warcraft: Forever coming on November 4, I'm thinking that maybe it's time to change that—to jump in and sample the greatest MMO as it was back then, but also new and improved. I'm not a GTA guy and there's not a whole hell of a lot else happening this month, so now's the time, right? Maybe. We'll see. — Andy Chalk

One bug deleted system files when you tried to uninstall the game. 25 years later, remaster factory SNEG promises it will actually work and I can finally find out if it's any good. — Jody Macgregor

Steam | November 9 Murky water scares me to death, but I also love the rush of giddy terror, so that's why the haunted fishing excursion awaiting in Dreadmoor has my full attention. I talk a big game, but I know I'm smashing ALT+F4 the second something lunges at me from the water. Until then, I'm looking forward to its early access debut. — Andrea Shearon

I was really hungover, but enjoyed seeing the island anyway, which says a lot, because it was a bumpy ride for someone who had recently stumbled out of a Big Lebowski-themed bar. What I'm saying is that this Euro Truck Sim 2 DLC seems like a pleasant way to get away from it all. 2— Tyler Wilde

Daniel Sikkema Sentenced to Life for Murder of Art Dealer Brent Sikkema

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The 56-year-old man is accountable for a "cold, calculated murder-for-hire plot," said US Attorney Jamie McDonald.

Daniel Sikkema, 56, has been sentenced to life in prison for his role in the murder-for-hire of his estranged husband, New York art dealer Brent Sikkema, the United States Attorney's Office for the Southern District of New York and the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) announced today, November 4.

Brent Sikkema, the 75-year-old co-owner of the Manhattan gallery Sikkema Jenkins & Company, was found dead in his Rio de Janeiro residence in January 2024. He was stabbed 18 times. Two months after the incident, Daniel Sikkema was arrested in Manhattan on charges of passport fraud and later received additional counts of murder-for-hire, conspiracy to commit murder for hire, and conspiracy to murder a person in a foreign country. A federal jury found him guilty on those charges following a two-week trial in May.

"Today’s life sentence holds Daniel Sikkema accountable for a cold, calculated murder-for-hire plot that resulted in the murder of his husband, Brent Sikkema,” US Attorney Jamie McDonald said in a press release. Daniel Sikkema was also sentenced to five years of supervised release.

“For months, the defendant manipulated friends, family, and employees to execute a killing driven by greed. His actions demonstrated a profound disregard for human life and the rule of law," McDonald continued.

Daniel Sikkema had paid the couple's former security guard, Alejandro Prevez, to kill his husband as their divorce proceedings dragged on. Shortly after the fatal stabbing, Daniel Sikkema sent $5,000 to Prevez and promised future payments. The former security guard was arrested by Brazilian law enforcement four days after the murder and still awaits charges in Brazilian courts, according to the press release.

Brent Sikkema co-founded Sikkema Malloy Jenkins (then called Sikkema Jenkins Gallery) with his business partner, Michael Jenkins, in the early 1990s, where he showed artists including Kara Walker, Sheila Hicks, and Vik Muniz. The gallery currently represents more than 30 artists.

A year in, Smartsheet CEO Raj Singh bets on a new vision beyond the company’s flagship product

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 4, 2026 at 3:31 pmNovember 4, 2026 at 5:53 pm

Smartsheet’s biggest new product in years doesn’t require Smartsheet.

The Bellevue, Wash.-based company on Wednesday unveiled vsActual, as in “plan versus actual,” an AI product that reads a company’s strategy and budgets, tracks the work across tools such as Jira, Workday and Slack, and flags when projects drift off course.

It’s the first standalone product under CEO Raj Singh, the Concur co-founder who took over Smartsheet a year ago, and it’s aimed at CEOs, CFOs and COOs rather than the project managers who have long been Smartsheet’s core users.

At the company’s Engage conference in Seattle, Singh showed a slide from a year ago labeling Smartsheet “a product company,” with the company and its software in a single box.

“Smartsheet, the company, and Smartsheet, the product, were exactly the same thing — same box, same ceiling — an attractive ceiling, but still a ceiling,” he said. Now, he explained, Smartsheet is a multi-product company, with vsActual as a key milestone.

Singh was relaxed and at home on the stage, engaging warmly with the crowd and his fellow presenters like someone who’d been running Smartsheet for years. He went off script often enough that he paused at one point to reassure the teleprompter operator everything was OK, and his talk ultimately ran so long that it threw off the morning’s schedule.

In an interview with GeekWire after the keynote, Singh said the company has more room to grow by working with the software that customers already use than by trying to move everything into its flagship product.

“Most of the competition is saying, ‘Well, if you move all your stuff onto my platform, you’ll be totally in control,’” he said. “I think every CIO is thinking, I’m never going to do that.”

Founded two decades ago, Smartsheet is best known for spreadsheet-style software that teams use to plan projects, track tasks and automate routine work.

It went public in 2018 and was taken private in January 2025 by Vista Equity Partners and Blackstone in an $8.4 billion deal, when it had more than $1 billion in annual revenue. Its rivals include Microsoft, Google and Salesforce, as well as Asana, Monday.com, Airtable and ClickUp.

Singh described vsActual as part of a broader product lineup that also includes Smartsheet’s flagship software and Brandfolder, the digital asset management service the company acquired in 2020.

All three draw on the same underlying data, built from 20 years of tracking how work gets planned, approved and done in Smartsheet, which the company calls its Execution Graph. Future products will be built the same way, Singh said.

“The Execution Graph is the long-term differentiated value of our company,” he said.

A change in pricing: Smartsheet is also moving away from charging by the user. Under a new consumption-based model, customers pay a base fee plus credits that are drawn down as people use the software, including its AI features, automations and API connections. Seat-based plans remain available.

Singh said customers balked at paying full price for people who use Smartsheet only occasionally. As an example, he said creating a dashboard might cost 50 cents, rather than a license that runs hundreds of dollars. Customers who switch won’t be charged for going over their credits, though he didn’t say for how long.

He said the net effect on revenue should be “nothing but positive,” describing the shift as a way to unlock demand from customers who held back under per-seat pricing.

“Agents are clearly going to be users moving forward,” he added.

Competition and risks: The new product enters a crowded field. Microsoft, ServiceNow, Workday and others are adding AI agents that plan and track work inside their own products, and AI-native startups are going after the same status-report busywork.

The software also depends on data from systems run by other companies, including rivals such as Asana. Singh said Smartsheet connects to them through APIs and the Model Context Protocol (MCP), an open standard for linking AI models to outside software, depending on how deep the integration goes.

The approach carries some risk: if companies can track their work through vsActual without moving it into Smartsheet, they have less reason to expand their use of the core software, the company’s main business.

Asked about that, Singh pointed to new AI features in Smartsheet itself, including its Smart Assist agent and an MCP server that lets customers use Smartsheet from AI assistants such as Claude and Copilot. “The core keeps growing,” he said.

Keith Kirkpatrick, a Futurum Group analyst, wrote Wednesday that vsActual is “the company’s clearest move yet from managing tasks to governing strategy,” but said the strategy requires Smartsheet to sell to a different kind of buyer.

“Its sellers are also not used to finance-led deals with long evaluation cycles,” he wrote, adding that many executives still see Smartsheet as a “glorified Excel” project management tool.

Singh said Smartsheet’s sales force already knows how to sell to large enterprises, but acknowledged that the company will need people who understand corporate planning. It will build a team of vsActual specialists, as it has for Brandfolder.

Kirkpatrick also noted that vsActual requires a company to work from one official plan, unlike Smartsheet’s more flexible core software, and said the company will need to show that its Execution Graph offers the same depth for customers whose work lives mostly in other systems.

“Decoupling widens the market but strains the main differentiator,” he wrote.

Smartsheet hasn’t said when vsActual will be generally available or how it will be priced. It’s building the product with 12 enterprise design partners, including some of the world’s largest food service, financial services and health systems, Singh said.

From Concur to Smartsheet: Singh started Concur in the early 1990s with his brother, Steve Singh, and Mike Hilton, and the company grew into a top provider of corporate travel and expense software before SAP acquired it for $8.3 billion in 2014.

Raj Singh later led health benefits company Accolade through its IPO and sale, and took over as Smartsheet CEO in October 2025, after longtime CEO Mark Mader retired and Apptio co-founder Sunny Gupta served as acting CEO.

Smartsheet cut jobs that month and again in February. Singh has since rebuilt the leadership team, including several executives he worked with at Concur and Accolade.

Singh said the leadership team is now fully assembled and the company is hiring, though AI means headcount won’t grow the way it once did.

He didn’t disclose revenue but said the business is growing, especially internationally and with government customers, and that retention remains strong despite fears that AI would lead companies to abandon traditional software. More than 100 customers now pay Smartsheet at least $1 million a year, a record, he said.

He credited Smartsheet’s founders and past leaders with building a successful tool for project managers, calling former CEO Mark Mader and co-founder Brent Frei friends.

Singh said the bigger opportunity is to help businesses run better, and he sees no reason Smartsheet can’t become one of the most important software companies in the world.

“I’m 58 years old,” he said. “I’m not doing this because I need to make an extra buck. I’m doing this because I think there’s a very important company in here, and we just have to unearth it, and then we have to get to customers and make them see that new vision.”

In just a few weeks, AWS re:Invent brings advanced and expert-level sessions on the services you build on every day, from serverless and containers to databases and developer tools. Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.

🥾 Further Than I’ve Gone Before 🌄

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The cloudless night and new moon would have been a good night to cowboy camp.

Waking up to birds singing, the morning was peaceful and cold. Struggling to get out of my warm quilt, I walked to the stream to collect water.

With my leg a little sore, I crawled back into my tent. My food bag was feeling light, i made oatmeal and tea for breakfast.

As Specs, then Sunshine, walked by, we greeted each other. Later I was cleaning my pot, as NBC, All-Star, and Joker hiked past.

It was almost 10 a.m. before I left camp. I’m an idiot. I could already feel the heat of the day.

As I hiked, the volume of my music kept increasing, and I was unable to tell what was causing it. Annoyed, I pushed through some overgrown bushes, then restarted my phone.

A raven flew through the clear blue sky. The desert was absolutely stunning, with purple, white, and yellow flowers blooming on green plants against the tan sand.

As I started my 1,400-foot descent, Sunshine was sitting on a big, flat rock in the shade. I joined her for a minute and took off my shoes. I dug through my pack, finding a bluetooth dongle.  I had taken it out of my selfie stick tripod when I put it in the hiker box back in Tehachapi. Sunshine offered me a half-liter of water, which I gratefully accepted.

Down at Kelso Valley Road, about 15 full water jugs sat along a fence. The blue five-gallon jugs were hot from the sun. With no shade, Sunshine, All-Star, Joker, and NBC all stopped for water, then left while I stayed, hoping for a place to blog while I waited out the heat of the day. An SUV stopped and rolled its windows down to take a picture of the PCT sign and trail. The four men were in the area checking on an emergency glider landing strip, and one of them had hiked on the PCT before. They asked if I needed anything. “I’m fine, but if you have any extra snacks, I would take them.” In a few minutes, I was holding two peanut butter and jelly sandwiches (with crunchy PB), a packet of trail mix, an apple, and a hard-boiled egg. After they drove off, I felt very alone in the middle of the desert.

I watched a bunny hop by, then a hawk fly over as I ate the apple and both peanut butter sandwiches. I had 3.75 liters of water in my backpack. I mixed coffee in a bottle and hiked out at 5:40 p.m.

Passing a trail register that had seen better days. I had fifteen exposed desert miles to the next water cache.

I sighed deeply. My shin felt a little sore. This was the last section I hiked in 2018. Maybe the pain was all in my head.

Pushing through the sand after a few miles, I saw Sunshine at her camp, tucked near a Joshua tree. She was surrounded by little purple flowers scattered around.

We traded half her dinner for my solar panel (I kept forgetting about it anyway). Sitting in the sand, I ate the food while we chatted.

As I hiked into the setting sun, little white moths were flying around the blooming plants.

As the sun set, I realized how tired I was. I crossed Spring Canyon Road and found a windy flat spot to set up my tent.

With no rocks to help hold my tent stakes in the sand, I hoped the tent would hold. Dirty, sweaty, and greasy from sunscreen, I lay down without changing. Exhausted, I passed out.

Waking up, I was surprised my tent had not collapsed in the wind. I was packed and on trail at 4:30 a.m. The waning crescent moon was smiling down on me. Other than navigating a couple of washouts, I was able to hike by the glow of the moon. This is an absolutely beautiful section of the trail.

I was hiking along the ridgeline when the sun began to reveal sweeping views of the desert valley. I feel grateful to be out here hiking this trail.

At 8:00 a.m., I passed Bird Spring Pass Trailhead, where an empty Jeep was parked. The water cache was almost identical to the previous one. Originally started and maintained by legendary hiker Devilfish to aid hikers in this dry 35-mile stretch. The caches are now maintained locally by legendary trail angels Mike and Terry.

Filtering water, I sat in a small patch of shade. I watched a hare hop across the road as I ate oatmeal, drank coffee, and ate a hard-boiled egg with some habanero garlic hot sauce.

My food bag feeling light as I stuffed it into my backpack, preparing for the 1,500-foot exposed climb out of the valley.

At 8:40 a.m., Worms, Sea Dog, Double Down, and Specs arrived at the cache.

At 9:25 a.m., I began this new stretch of trail, excited to hit 10 miles by 10:00 a.m.!

An hour and a half later, I reached the top of the valley, immediately taking an hour break.

The heat of the day hit me as I left the shade of my lunch spot, partially regretting such a long break.

The sandy mountainside was speckled with little purple and yellow flowers.

Hearing helicopters, I looked across the valley to see a black scar with a red outline in the distance. Fire season had already started.

Hitting a wall, I stopped at a shady camp spot, taking a nap. With over 5 miles left and only a few sips of water in my bottle, I made one more push.

Hiking on an exposed service road, I saw lizards and horned toads brought out by the heat. I’m glad the day was starting to cool off.

An adorable baby cottontail bunny hopped out in front of me on the road. It was so tiny. I stopped to watch it for a second.

At 6:30 p.m., I dropped my pack on the porch of McIver’s cabin.

Crossing to the other side of the pipe spring, I filled my water bottle and spotted a flat place to set up my tent.

Tired enough to cowboy camp, I didn’t want to be annoyed by bugs, so I set up my tent.

Exhausted, I made hot tea and forced myself eat dinner. After all, it was a 21-mile day.

Unable to get comfortable all night, I tossed and turned while my sleeping pad slid to the bottom of my tent.

Waking up at 4:30 a.m., I was thankful that at least the stars were beautiful.

I was tired and hungry with had no oatmeal or coffee left. Eating some trail mix as I hiking down the road at 7 a.m., I had a handful of Skittles, a half cup of trail mix, two Clif Bars, and one electrolyte, along with the electrolyte in my water.

Ridge walking toward Walker Pass, I stopped in the shade to apply sunscreen just before the trail curved. I enjoyed the beautiful views of the desert valley and the Sierras beyond, although it was smoggy, possibly from fires.

As I was signing the trail log at the junction to Walker Pass Campground, I heard voices. Turning around, I saw All-Star, Joker, and NBC walking down the trail. They were rushing to catch the bus into Ridgecrest. Reaching the bus stop together, we decided to drop our backpacks and hitchhike while waiting for the bus.

A driver in a small SUV pulled over. With the passenger seat already filled, we were surprised when all four of us fit in the back seat. Our packs in the small cargo area.

The valley floor of the Mojave Desert was hot in the afternoon sun. Being dropped off at Starbucks, we thanked our new friends and slung our packs. The four of us walked off in search of lunch. A mile down the bright, shadeless city sidewalk, we walked into Saigon Flavor (a Vietnamese restaurant), where we were welcomed, hiker stink and all. We talked until our food arrived, then silence as we gobbled up our meals.

Full, we walked next door to the Orient Outlet. I purchased coffee candy, a mango, and a coconut water.

The afternoon heat was unbearable. When we were unable to find a rideshare, NBC went back into the restaurant. She returned with our server, Lilliana, who offered to drive all four of us to our destinations.

Arriving at the address given to me, I saw trekking poles and boots outside. I knew I was at the correct address. Inside were Sherpa and Nobody, along with Oliver the poodle, Hopper the terrier, and the calico cat Luna.

I was offered vegetables from the table: broccoli, carrots, radishes, and cauliflower with a sour cream dip.

I spent some time socializing even though I was stressed about having time to blog and other chores while in town.

Nobody made a delicious spaghetti dinner with bread and side salad to share with our new friends, TA Jellyfish and Burn Out. It was nice sitting down together and enjoying a home-cooked meal after a long day on the trail.

I soaked my foot in Epsom salt to get the sand out. Before bed, I spent some time going through the hiker box. I took the snow baskets off a pair of trekking poles and grabbed a few stickers. Never know what you’ll find in a hiker box!

Waking up refreshed. A sliver of light sneaking into the dark room from behind the curtains. How grateful I am for this morning. Wearing clean clothes, sleeping in a clean bed.

I opened the bedroom door to be warmly greeted by Oliver and Hopper. Nobody was in the kitchen scratching up some breakfast. Sipping on coffee, we chatted over our heavenly meal of poached eggs, homestyle potatoes topped with sour cream and parsley, with fruit on the side.

Nobody let me pick through his food bag. This came in handy during today’s resupply mission.

Retreating to the dark bedroom, I spent some time on my writing.

Leftover spaghetti for lunch, then off to run some errands.

Meeting Sunshine to pick up my solar panel, then heading to the post office to mail home some extra gear and stickers. I picked up a few snacks for my food bag and a gallon of milk to drink.

Sandy had some gear in the back of her car on its way to the hiker box. I took out six Pachallama 2g Carbon-Pin Tent Stakes. I can’t wait to try these out!

Nobody rehashed the spaghetti into a bake with cottage cheese. Another delicious meal! I’m going to miss him being around.

After dinner, I posted on my Instagram while drinking hot tea and soaking my feet. Finished with today’s chores, I crawled into my dark cave of a bed.

Today I am thankful to have met Nobody. To call a man with his earthy energy my friend. He is a kind soul who shared his wisdom about finding happiness in life. I’m also thankful for Sandy, aka Wings a true trail angel in Ridgecrest where I felt right at home the moment I stepped in the door.

First: Volvo CE Electric equipment does the work, without disturbing the wildlife

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

For the first time, electric equipment assets are being put to work in the National Wildlife Refuge System, where a battery-powered, zero-emission ECR25 Electric compact excavator and L25 Electric compact wheel loader made by Volvo CE are making the wild more accessible to everyone.

Construction work isn’t exactly welcome in the wilderness – between diesel exhaust, engine noises, and the constant rumble of heavy machinery, even a relatively small project can have a big impact on the native wildlife. To minimize that impact, US Fish and Wildlife Service is turning to Volvo CE and its compact excavators and wheel loaders to get the job done, building out a new, ADA accessible trail in Virginia’s Occoquan Bay National Wildlife Refuge.

“We are excited to partner with the US Fish and Wildlife Service on this project to demonstrate Volvo’s commitment to lowering emissions and working to build the future we all want and need,” said Dr. Ray Gallant, VP of Product Management and Productivity, Volvo CE. “Projects like the one at the Occoquan Bay National Wildlife Refuge give a glimpse of how electric machines can create a more climate-friendly future for the construction industry.”

The refuge is home to a diverse array of grasslands, wetlands, and woods that attract red foxes, painted turtles, osprey, river otters, and countless other rarely seen species – and the ADA accessible path being laid down by the L25 Electric compact wheel loader will allow wheelchair-bound visitors to travel a half-mile-long trail around one of the pond. Meanwhile, the ECR25 Electric compact excavator will be digging trenches to improve drainage from the pond and helping to place a viewing platform that extends out over the water.

Keeping the machines running is a portable EV ARC solar-powered charging system from Beam Global that’s designed to drive down the project’s carbon footprint even further.

NASA's Swift Space Telescope Re-Enters Atmosphere With Five Times More Risk Of Casualties Than Anticipated

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

On Wednesday, NASA's Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory, a space telescope that's been in orbit since 2004, finally re-entered Earth's atmosphere above Indonesia. However, it appears to have done so with a risk of casualties five times beyond what NASA technical standards mandate. How and why this happened isn't immediately clear; NASA Public Affairs Specialist Alise Fisher told Jalopnik, "In the leadup to Swift's Nov. 20, 2004, launch, NASA determined the risk was consistent with mission requirements as allowed by agency policy." Somewhere between launch and reentry, that risk assessment shifted. It's unlikely that anybody made a specific mistake; this is probably the result of, well, space being hard. Still, it does highlight that our main defense against the detritus of astronomical ambitions is pure luck.

Swift is only crashing to Earth because it ran out of fuel to stay in orbit or adjust its trajectory. To NASA's credit, it did throw $30 million at a private company in an unprecedented attempt to rescue Swift with another satellite that would pull it back up into safe orbit. Sadly, the rescue satellite itself failed, dooming Swift to this fate.

In formal terms, Swift suffered an "uncontrolled reentry," which basically means it had no propulsion and just fell from space. NASA's own requirements, most recently updated in 2021 but going all the way back to 2001, state: "For uncontrolled reentry, the risk of human casualty from surviving debris shall be less than 0.0001 (1:10,000)." If you're not aware, there's no actual law requiring that satellites de-orbit in some sort of safe way that won't, you know, crash into a city or kill anyone. So our shield against the skies is just statistics. This is the threshold at which NASA feels the risk is acceptable. For reference, in all of history, just about 10,000 satellites have de-orbited, so... statistically we're due. And that was before we were putting tens of thousands of satellites up every few years.

In Swift's instance, however, NASA played its cards a little close to its chest. For one thing, it did not put out a risk assessment for the reentry, which is unusual. Then, in the middle of a longer announcement, the agency mentioned that Swift's risk of human casualties was actually "1 in 2,100." That's a risk of human injury or death five times beyond what it was meant to be.

Fortunately, the odds appear to have resolved in our favor. There are no reports of anyone being struck by space debris from Swift. Most of the satellite was expected to burn up during reentry; the trouble would come from whatever survived, likely dense pieces like the telescope lens or the reaction wheels. If any of those made it to the surface, there doesn't appear to have been a person around it.

That's a roll of the dice, though. At some point, as thousands of satellites de-orbit per year in our new megaconstellation era, laws should be passed to manage this formally. That would likely mean requiring controlled reentries, when the satellite still has a little fuel left to navigate to a safe spot out in the ocean. The other option, which companies like SpaceX have been pursuing, is to design the satellite to burn up entirely on reentry. The issue there is what effect all that vaporized metal might have on the atmosphere. Scientists don't yet know the answer, for the simple reason that there was never a reason to ask that question before. Now, the health of our planet may depend on it.

As space gets busier and busier, more and more will fall to us down here from up there. Up until now, the power of statistics has kept us safe. But eventually, a 1 in 10,000 chance is going to happen. Especially if some of those chances turn out to be riskier than intended.

Bari Weiss Opens Up About Her Biggest Objective For CBS News: ‘That Is First And Foremost The Goal’

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

How A CBS News Insider Responded After Dan Rather Criticized The Company

A Former Fox News Meteorologist Is Not Holding Back About '#MeanGirl' Megyn Kelly

I Didn’t See Sophia Bush Comparing Her Time On Chicago P.D. To Megyn Kelly's At Fox News Coming

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Much has been said about Weiss' leadership.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Bari Weiss’ public profile rose considerably back in October 2025 when David Ellison named her the editor-in-chief of CBS News. Since then, a number of pundits – those who’ve worked for the Eye Network and those who haven’t – have been weighing in on Weiss’ approach to the job. Although some have spoken out in support of the journalist/political commentator, others have criticized several of the decisions she’s made. Now, Weiss is opening up about her biggest goal for the company.

Just recently, Weiss took part in a public discussion held by Information, a technology-centric news outlet. It was during that conversation that she discussed various topics, including the concerns regarding 60 Minutes’ recent ratings decrease. Weiss was asked directly about her vision for CBS News after having been outspoken about wanting to act as a voice for “the 75% that aren’t served.” Weiss then spoke about navigating news in this social and political landscape before saying that her vision was built on one detail – trust:

Everyone in this room knows that we’re living in an era of collapsing trust. And so that is first and foremost the goal – build a news organization, a mass news organization that is deserving of trust. I think, a lot of times people talk about, ‘We have a crisis in trust.’ No, we have a crisis in trustworthiness. A lot of media organizations, especially in the mainstream, especially over the past decade, either ignored or misconstrued major stories that ere of incredible, national, even international significance. And so that is the reality, that is the landscape in which I’m building CBS.

How Bari Weiss Responded After Being Asked If She’d Like To Run CNN

Trust has been a significant topic of discussion regarding CBS News since Weiss began running the outlet. Amid her tenure, there have been claims of editorial partiality, with some former employees claiming they were pressured to insert what they considered to be bias into their reporting. Weiss herself has denied the allegations of “journalistic interference” and, based on the comments she shared at the recent discussion (via YouTube) she’s looking to build trustworthiness.

Alleged editorial bias isn’t the only reason some have taken issue with Weiss. Before being hired to join CBS, she founded an online media outlet, The Free Press, which was purchased by the now-defunct Paramount Skydance. Former 60 Minutes alum Scott Pelley – who was fired for allegedly accusing Weiss of “murdering” the show – has spoken about her apparent lack of experience in traditional broadcast journalism. Pelley argues that his former workplace is in need of “adult supervision.” Despite the issues with Weiss’ background, she seems keen on taking elements of both traditional and non-traditional practices:

The second thing, and we’ve done this, I think, in a really powerful way at The Free Press — obviously CBS is totally different scale — is how do you marry the energy and vibrancy and vitality of the independent creator world, for lack of a better term, and marry it to the rigor and standards of great news organizations like the ones that we work for?

While Pelley and veteran broadcaster Dan Rather have expressed disapproval of Weiss’ work, some of her current employees have come to her defense. Current CBS Evening News anchor Tony Dokoupil said he hadn’t experienced any news bias and spoke positively of Weiss’ leadership. Sir Trevor Phillips – CBS News’ newly hired senior global affairs correspondent and 60 Minutes contributor – also defended Weiss’ running of the brand. Phillips made note of his boss’ attempts to build an effective, new team moving forward.

With the media landscape continuing to evolve, it’s likely that CBS News will have to further confront changes in one way or another. Time will tell, of course, how Bari Weiss and co. choose to respond to shifts in the industry.

Will Expedia's advertising on Meta's Muse and Google AI Overviews be the new Google Search? Ariane Gorin is hopeful, citing Expedia proven track record in these sorts of deals. On the other hand, in years' past Expedia officials complained about the high cost of Google marketing.

How will big AI platforms perform as customer acquisition channels for Expedia Group and other online travel agencies, and what will they cost?

"The economics of the partnerships are being worked out," Expedia Group CEO Ariane Gorin said Wednesday about Meta's Muse and Google's AI Overviews. "You know, we have a track record of being able to work with big advertising platforms or tech platforms to use them as channels to acquire customers at attractive economics, and that's what I think we'll see."

Gorin said on Expedia’s third-quarter earnings

Expedia Group used its third-quarter earnings call to describe AI platforms as a customer acquisition channel still being priced. CEO Ariane Gorin said the economics of partnerships with Meta's Muse and Google's AI Overviews are "being worked out," and pointed to the company's history of acquiring customers through large advertising and tech platforms at attractive economics. She said Expedia is leading in citation share in the U.S. — how often its brands are cited as a source by AI chatbots — and that an integration with Muse was imminent. Gorin said the integrations are a way to learn how agents make decisions and which customers use them, and that travelers using agentic AI for discovery still want to book with a trusted brand. The underlying numbers were strong: revenue rose 12% to $4.9 billion, net income rose 7% to $1.03 billion, and room nights grew 7%, matching Booking Holdings. B2B revenue climbed 23% to $1.7 billion while the consumer brands grew 6%. Expedia raised full-year gross bookings guidance to 10% growth from 8-9%, and revenue to 11-12% from 9-10%. CFO Derek Andersen said the fourth-quarter environment is softer than the third, citing disruptions in the Middle East and unrest in some European cities.

Slavia Prague boss Jindrich Trpisovsky issues Arsenal verdict after Champions League defeat

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Slavia Prague head coach Jindrich Trpisovsky has said Arsenal gave his side a “footballing lesson” during their Champions League showdown.

First half goals from Noni Madueke and Viktor Gyokeres ultimately proved to be the difference for Arsenal on Wednesday night as they left the Fortuna Arena with all three points for a second season in a row.

However, the Gunners simply did not have things their own way and were left hanging on at the end when Wiktor Nowak pulled a goal back for the hosts in second half stoppage time.

Prior to that, Gabriel was forced into a desperate clearance under his own bar, David Raya produced one sensational save to tip a header onto the post and Slavia Prague had a claim for a penalty checked and cleared by VAR.

Reflecting on the defeat, which saw Slavia become the first team in Champions League history to not win for 23 matches, Trpisovsky heaped praise on the Premier League champions but felt his half time changes made a massive difference.

“At half-time, we had a long and honest discussion,” he told UEFA. “The substitutions worked well, and the change in formation made a difference. We started the second half strongly. In the first half, we were too far away from our opponents and Arsenal had a clear edge.

“They gave us a footballing lesson, but after the break we got much closer to them. We fought for every ball, competed hard and played with real intensity.

"We all felt that the second half was much better. We got a goal, and I believed we would create a few more chances. I'm pleased that we were able to give the fans something to celebrate with that goal.

"These are hugely valuable experiences for us. In the first half, we showed too much respect for the quality of our opponents. The second half, however, showed us the way forward and gave us a blueprint for future matches."

Mercado Bitcoin says phased reopening to begin Monday, will cover losses

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Customers will initially be able to view their holdings, while withdrawals and trading remain suspended pending security checks.

Brazilian crypto exchange Mercado Bitcoin said it will begin restoring customer account access Monday after a hack that blockchain analysts estimated resulted in more than $46 million in stolen assets.

In a statement to Cointelegraph, Mercado Bitcoin said customers would initially regain access through its website to view balances, statements, holdings and investments. Transfers, withdrawals and trading will resume in later phases, subject to independent security testing.

Mercado Bitcoin also pledged to use company funds to replenish assets affected by the breach, saying more than $15 million in stolen funds had already been frozen with help from the industry.

“Our trading desk is preparing to begin the necessary repurchases in domestic and international markets,” it said.

The update follows the exchange’s disclosure of a security breach on Monday, which saw attackers transferring funds from the company’s hot wallets and customer deposit addresses through standard transfers.

Addressing liquidity, Mercado said it had a strong cash position, bolstered by a $20 million investment from stablecoin issuer Tether to support operational expansion.

Farioli in conferenza stampa: “Il Napoli ha preparato la gara in maniera intelligente, match importante”

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Francesco Farioli, allenatore del Porto, ha rilasciato alcune dichiarazioni in conferenza stampa dopo la vittoria in Champions League contro il Napoli:  “Il gioco è stato molto pesante, ci sono stati diversi momenti in cui siamo stati molto energetici e coraggiosi, soprattutto quando siamo andati a prendere la palla e loro ci pressavano, avevamo la giusta mentalità come i tifosi e questo ci ha aiutato ad avere la prestazione avuta. In ogni intervista ho detto che è stata una buona Champions ma non avevamo avuto punti, stavolta abbiamo raccolto i frutti. Questa vittoria è importante perchè ci servivano punti, sappiamo la storia del club e l’esigenza di fare bene in Champions League, di ottenere risultati. Una cosa non si può dire, che ci sia mancanza di volontà: ogni tre giorni abbiamo le giuste motivazioni, ci sono sempre gli avversari e le partite da giocare, e non sempre siamo al top della forma e a certi livelli la pressione si fa sentire. Dobbiamo imparare sulla nostra pelle, ci prendiamo i tre punti contro una squadra eccellente e ora vediamo le ultime quattro partite per guadagnare più punti possibili. La partita di stasera e i risultati hanno cambiato la prospettiva del match, abbiamo dovuto cambiare interpretazione del match e il coraggio dei ragazzi è stato notevole. Siamo stati bravissimi nella verticalizzazione e nel secondo tempo, l’impatto delle sostituzioni è stato fondamentale, chi è entrato ha contributo tanto. Cosa non è andato bene alla fine del primo tempo? In Champions ogni punto conta così come ogni gol può fare la differenza, stasera vincere con tre gol di differenza sicuramente è importante e ci aiuta ad avanzare, la performance dei ragazzi è stata eccellente. Le difficoltà del primo tempo? Non sapevamo cosa avrebbero fatto, avevamo preparato cinque scenari possibili e stasera abbiamo visto le scelte degli avversari con giocatori che non ci aspettavamo in campo. Abbiamo reagito bene e fatto la differenza. Come ho visto il Napoli? Non per dare meriti ai miei, ma il Napoli ha preparato la partita in maniera intelligente, quando ho visto la formazione ho pensato come potessero creare. La prima lettura è stata una scelta strategica e ben pensata in pochi minuti, abbiamo cercato di aggiustare le nuove dinamiche con l’ingresso di De Bruyne sotto punta, siamo stati bravi a portare dentro tanta energia, nel secondo tempo siamo venuti fuori con la condizione fisica e ritmo, chi è entrato ha alzato il ritmo ed è qualcosa in cui credo molto. Ho chiesto a Fofana e Gimenez di giocare fuoriruolo ma hanno avuto determinazione e spirito. Il calcio italiano? Le informazioni sono veloci e si può raffrontarsi con altre realtà, la Serie A la seguo da tifoso e da osservatore esterno, alcune dinamiche le conoscete meglio voi. Nuova onda degli allenatori toscani? Facciamo un lavoro bellissimo quando i risultati vanno bene, difficilissimo se la palla non entra. Spalletti ed Allegri sono dei riferimenti del calcio italiano, hanno portato innovazione nel ruolo dell’allenatore e ho un rispetto enorme per ciò che hanno fatto e continuano a fare. Su Alvini posso dire che è incredibile per lo spirito che ha il Frosinone, tanti anni fa andavo a vedere i suoi allenamenti al Tuttocuoio e ha sempre avuto una grande passione. Mi ha aperto le porte del suo centro sportivo per gli allenamenti e ancora non pensavo di allenare, ha un modo contagioso per come allena. Come abbiamo vinto? A fine primo tempo ho detto di avere ancora fiducia come nei primi 35′ di partita. Abbiamo avuto coraggio e alla lunga abbiamo meritato la vittoria, grande meriti ai ragazzi. Gabri Veiga aveva la febbre, aveva una faccia bianca stamattina e non aveva dormito: quando è uscito gli ho detto meglio fare così ogni partita. Ha grande intelligenza, conosce i momenti della gara e oltre a leggere il gioco aggiunge qualcosa di speciale come la sua fisicità strabordante come volume ed esplosività. Ha ancora grandi margini di miglioramento sulla continuità, sta migliorando e rispetto al suo percorso ha voglia di fare tanto e bene. Poi tra cinque anni chissà, per ora ce lo godiamo. Occasione per un italiano all’estero prima di tornare in Italia? A volte non danno e a volte non si prendono le opportunità, ci sono momenti: negli ultimi due anni e mezzo ho lavorato per club con sale piene di trofei. Lavorare assieme come succede al Porto poi ti rende difficile guardare altrove, sono felice qui: è la realtà, sono felice di lavorare con un gruppo straordinario e con le persone che rappresentano il club e fanno di tutto per il club, creativi nel cercare soluzioni nei momenti di difficoltà. Abbiamo tante partite da giocare, testa bassa e pedalare”.

What Wall Street billionaires are saying about Democrats' midterm sweep

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

America's billionaires woke up Wednesday to a political reckoning — and a Wall Street rally.

As Democrats celebrated a sweeping midterm victory, some of the country's wealthiest donors traded barbs over record election spending. Others warned the results could threaten the artificial intelligence boom.

Wall Street, however, cheered the outcome. The Dow Jones Industrial Average soared 1,569 points, or 3%, while the S&P 500 gained 1.6% and the Nasdaq Composite rose 1.4%, as investors welcomed political gridlock, reduced uncertainty and President Donald Trump's willingness to accept the results.

Virgin Group founder Richard Branson took aim at the billions spent influencing the election.

"I have felt embarrassed by the billions that tech entrepreneurs and hedge fund managers have spent," Branson wrote Wednesday on LinkedIn, calling for campaign finance reform. Branson did not give donations that met the threshold reporting in the 2026 election cycle.

His comments came as Elon Musk, the election's largest individual donor, watched his preferred Republicans lose the Texas Senate and governor races despite pouring $224 million into the state — nearly half his record $474 million in political giving nationwide, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records.

Early Wednesday, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO suggested on X that data centers in space were looking increasingly appealing before joking that his "election-interfering space lasers" had failed.

LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, another major political donor, took a sharper tone.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote late Tuesday on X, accusing Musk of spending "outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power" while defending his own contributions to Democrats.

Other Democratic megadonors celebrated. "The world changed yesterday," Alex Soros wrote Wednesday on X, hailing the results as a generational victory after his family contributed roughly $103 million to Democratic political efforts.

But beyond the political sparring, some of the country's richest investors were focused on a potentially bigger consequence of the Democratic sweep: the future of the AI boom.

Even before polls closed Tuesday, Pershing Square founder Bill Ackman warned on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that slowing AI development could jeopardize America's technological edge. "Practically speaking, we can't slow down," he said.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, billionaire investor Chamath Palihapitiya sounded an even starker warning.

Palihapitiya initially questioned whether the early results would hold, but warned that if they did, the implications for the AI trade could be severe.

"And the only way to sink the AI trade is to sink the data centers," Palihapitiya said during an election-night livestream of the "All-In" podcast.

Texas offered an early test of those fears. Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, who has proposed a moratorium on new data-center construction until stronger safeguards are established, defeated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

During the same livestream, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner warned the Democratic wave could mean at least 10 to 15 gigawatts of expected AI data-center capacity would not come online in 2027. Wall Street analyst Dan Ives estimated Wednesday that 10% of planned U.S. data centers would no longer go ahead.

AMD CEO Lisa Su acknowledged the growing resistance to AI's expansion, urging technology companies to demonstrate the benefits of data centers to local communities.

"At the end of the day, what we need to do is ensure that everybody wins from AI," Su told CNBC Wednesday.

Yet Wall Street may have found opportunity in the same slowdown that worried AI's biggest backers.

Fewer new data centers could increase the value of existing facilities while easing the enormous spending burden on technology giants. Big Tech stocks surged Wednesday, adding more than $750 billion in market value at one point, even as smaller, debt-dependent data-center operators tumbled.

Jackson Olson Jokes About Impressing GF Maggie's Dad Pat Sajak on Vacation

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Savannah Bananas star Jackson Olson is winning over the entire Sajak family — one TikTok at a time.

“First trip with the Sajak fam = success … I think?” Olson, 29, wrote via TikTok on Tuesday, November 3, alongside footage with girlfriend Maggie Sajak and her famous dad, former Wheel of Fortune host Pat Sajak, lip-syncing a beloved Gilmore Girls scene.

“What do you have to offer?” Pat, 80, mouthed before Olson replied, “Nothing, only this,” and started dancing. (Sean Gunn’s Kirk Gleason originally said the now-viral “nothing” line in his character’s avant-garde short film, which premiered during a town movie night during season 2.)

After receiving a look from Pat, the Dancing With the Stars season 35 castoff buried his face in his hands and walked off camera.

Olson has been dating Maggie, 31, since April.

“It’s really cool because we’re obviously living very different lives right now on the opposite side of the country, but we’re able to come together and just have an awesome relationship,” Olson exclusively told Us in May. “I feel like in any relationship, you’re trying to figure out how to see each other as frequently as possible, which is never something that I thought in my past I was going to want to do … but now I really do.”

He continued at the time, “It’s just about planning and making sure you’re setting aside time to see each other and make really cool experiences happen because a start of a relationship never happens again. You only get one chance at a start of a relationship.”

While speaking with Us, Olson revealed that he already met Pat.

“He’s a great guy,” he gushed. “Everybody’s nervous to meet the father of the person they’re dating, or mother or both. I’m a very weird person, I’m a very out there person. I’m not going to hold back who I am as a person, and people seem to enjoy that company. I’m happy that I’m able to be myself truly in every aspect now.”

Olson also had Maggie’s support during his DWTS tenure.

“It’s so awesome [to have her here]. I try not to look at her during the dance because I know that she’s smiling,” he told Us in September, weeks before his Halloween Horror Nights elimination. “I know that she’s so excited and smiling. And if I looked at her, I would just like be like, ‘Ah!’ But it’s awesome, and she’s so supportive of both of us, too. She was wearing the Team Banana ballroom shirt today, so that was cool.”

The Blue Wave Has Tech Bros Scrambling for Data Centers in Space

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A wave of Democratic candidates across the country—and notably in Texas, a national data center hot spot and home to Elon Musk’s SpaceX—won their state and congressional races on Tuesday, including many candidates who voiced opposition to the data center industry. The results have sent some corners of the tech industry looking to the heavens for answers.

In recent hours, data center enthusiasts have proclaimed on social media that they’re still “bullish” on building data centers—just not necessarily in Texas, or even on Earth at all. Instead, they say, it’s time to focus on data centers in space.

Elon Musk—who spent more than $400 million of his fortune supporting Republican candidates who all went on to lose their midterms races—has been among those leading the charge. “Data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now,” Musk posted on X in the early hours of Wednesday, after it was clear that Democrats had taken the House and Senate.

Jordan Lasker—the man behind the X account "Crémieux,” which is a popular follow in the tech world and is known for posting race-based pseudoscience—also weighed in, saying, “Data centers in space are an incredible idea and anyone who doubted them didn't think enough about tonight's election results.”

Throughout the day, Musk employees, tech investors, AI researchers, venture capitalists, and industry fans joined the chorus of support for space-based data centers. Soham Mehta, a research fellow at the techno-optimist think tank Foundation for American Innovation, argued that underwater data centers should also be considered. (In June, China began operating what it claims is the world’s first underwater data center.)

Some companies are already trying to put compute in orbit. Among them are Google, which launched a satellite that it says contains “data center class TPU,” and SpaceX, which says it aims to deploy “thousands of AI satellites starting as soon as late 2027.” Blue Origin is also in the mix, having filed for a Federal Communications Commission permit to launch 50,000 satellites that will constitute an “orbital data center system.”

There are also a few startups aiming to build data centers in space, including Starcloud, which claims to have carried "the first Nvidia H100 GPU into orbit" in November 2025.

Despite being technically possible, the realities of in-orbit data centers are difficult. Among other things, space-based data centers are much more expensive than land-based ones, can’t grow to hyperscale size, and are subject to the risks and whims of satellite launches.

When it comes to Texas data centers, the concerns of tech bros aren’t completely unfounded. Experts tell WIRED that the change in leadership could translate to some concrete changes in the industry—albeit with limits.

Although Democrats will have control of the Texas House of Representatives, Republicans still have control over the state’s senate. Robert Paterson, professor of land use and environmental planning at the University of Texas at Austin, tells WIRED that the split means that some immediate attempts at change “will probably be stymied a bit.”

Adrian Shelley, director of nonprofit consumer advocacy group Public Citizen’s Texas office, tells WIRED that the legislature would be a likely place to hash out high-impact policies like the reallocation of state agency resources. However, he adds that the governor could effect more immediate changes by issuing executive orders, or requesting the resignation of politically appointed officials in state agencies.

Shelley says that these oversight agencies—such as the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality, the Public Utility Commission, and the Texas Water Development Board—have the authority to charge fines for breaking state law, or in extreme cases to revoke permits for projects like data centers. But Paterson notes that Texas developers have particularly strong “vested rights,” which protects the terms of their projects from the moment their permits are approved, even if construction hasn’t begun.

Another medium-term route to reshaping Texas’ data center industry could be to give more power to its counties. Paterson noted that nearly half of the state’s data centers exist in unincorporated territory, which puts them outside the jurisdiction of city zoning laws.

Unlike other states, Paterson says, Texas counties have relatively limited enforcement abilities. Under the state's so-called Dillon’s Rule, counties only have powers that are “specifically allotted to it by the state.” So while they have authority over things like access and egress to county roads or construction on floodplains, he says they don't have the zoning authority that would help closely regulate projects like data centers.

Paterson says that the new attorney general could theoretically adjust the terms in the state’s biannual handbook, which establishes the limits of county authority. He also argues that even in a split legislature, especially since anti-data-center sentiment crosses party lines, state lawmakers could propose a viable bill giving counties “limited zoning authority over data centers."

Shelley tells WIRED that since “data centers were a decisive factor in this blue wave,” the upcoming legislative season will be “very interesting.”

“I will say the data center industry hasn't shown a lot of interest in working with state lawmakers or local leaders,” Shelley says, “and I will be interested to see if that changes.”
